# AGENTS.md instructions for /Users/rstyczynski/projects/APEX_CICD

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# RUP Strikes Back Process

This project uses the `RUPStrikesBack` submodule as its agentic delivery method.

## Process entry point

The Codex method entry point is `.agents/skills/rup-strikes-back/SKILL.md`. Its references map Codex invocation to the canonical manager, agent-role, and rule procedures in `RUPStrikesBack/.claude/commands/` and `RUPStrikesBack/rules/`.

The root `BACKLOG.md` is the Product Owner’s prioritized backlog. The root `PLAN.md` is the sprint plan and identifies the active sprint. When present, `RUPStrikesBack.patch/` is an optional local wrapper; its root `RUP_patch.md` is the compatibility entry point for the full local policy at `RUPStrikesBack.patch/RUP_patch.md`.

For an explicit request to start, plan, inspect, or manage a RUP Strikes Back sprint in Codex, read and use `.agents/skills/rup-strikes-back/SKILL.md`. Do not load that workflow for ordinary product, documentation, or implementation requests.

## External expert review

For a request to start or continue an external expert review, use
`.agents/skills/rup-external-review/SKILL.md`. Codex coordinates the round,
prepares prompts for the Product Owner to relay, reads expert replies from
files and writes engineering responses and decisions. The skill owns the
external-review procedure; review alone does not start or close a sprint.

## Mandatory RUP artifact routing

RUP Strikes Back procedures are mandatory for every change to its managed
artifacts. Before changing `BACKLOG.md`, use
`.agents/skills/rup-backlog/SKILL.md`; before changing `PLAN.md` or a sprint
status, use `.agents/skills/rup-sprint/SKILL.md`; and before changing
`PROGRESS_BOARD.md`, use `.agents/skills/rup-progress/SKILL.md`. For an active
sprint cycle, use `.agents/skills/rup-strikes-back/SKILL.md` and the phase
adapters it selects.

When a request affects more than one managed artifact, read and follow every
applicable skill before editing. Do not infer a procedure from prior turns or
conversation context. If the canonical method and local patch do not state how
to proceed, stop and request a Product Owner decision; do not invent a state,
transition, or artifact format.

## Starting a sprint

Do not start a RUP cycle automatically. Start one only when the Product Owner explicitly requests it and the selected sprint has `Status: Progress` in the root `PLAN.md`.

Before executing the cycle:

1. When the optional wrapper is present, read `RUP_patch.md`, then `RUPStrikesBack.patch/RUP_patch.md`.
2. Read the Codex skill's manager adapter and only the phase adapters selected by the iteration profile.
3. Read the canonical submodule procedures identified by those adapters.
4. Apply the local wrapper only where it conflicts with or refines the generic method.

Codex does not natively register the submodule's Claude slash commands. An explicit request to use the `rup-strikes-back` skill for the active sprint is the Codex invocation.

## Scope

The submodule’s instructions describe the adopted delivery method. They do not authorize unrelated actions such as remote pushes, copying submodule policies wholesale, or changing project scope without a Product Owner request.

## Environment configuration and temporary files

By explicit Product Owner instruction, `.local-apex/` is disposable scratch only:
download caches, temporary checkouts, build outputs and diagnostic evidence. Never
use it as the authoritative store for environment settings, credentials, saved
connections, runner registration or executable-path environment files.

GitHub Actions must declare tool setup and configuration explicitly. Use GitHub
environment/repository variables for nonsecret settings and GitHub secrets for
credentials. Generate required private connection files and mappings in
`RUNNER_TEMP`, clean them on success or failure, and use `GITHUB_PATH`,
`GITHUB_ENV` or step outputs for generated job paths. Persistent runner
registration belongs in the runner's managed host directory outside the
checkout. Preserve historical evidence without presenting its retired local
configuration instructions as the current setup.

### Temporary prepared-host credential accommodation — 2026-11-04

The Product Owner explicitly approved retaining read-only lookup of the existing
E.1/E.2 `.local-apex/e1-secrets.json` record on the already prepared E.3 host.
This narrow R1-L1 deferral preserves requested Builder, author-schema and LAB_USER
password retrieval; it does not authorize creating new settings/credentials in
scratch, copying the record to another host, or using it in GitHub jobs. The
record is a retained dependency while this exception applies and must not be
discarded as disposable scratch. Do not move/delete it or introduce a new store.
GitHub environment secrets remain the job authority and native author profiles
remain outside checkout scratch. End the accommodation when a compliant source
remedy is selected; reconsider before new-host reproduction, scratch cleanup or
expanding this exception. See the [review decision](progress/E.3/sprint_E.3_handover_review-1-decisions.md#r1-l1--product-owner-policy-choice-required).
This is a temporary prepared-host exception, not production NFR-4 compliance.

## SQLcl PROJECT toolchain compatibility

Treat [SRS NFR-5](docs/srs.md#quality-requirements-and-initial-release) as a
production requirement. For every accepted Git state, the running SQLcl release
must match `.sqlcl.version` in `.dbtools/project.config.json`; developer and
pipeline processing must use the same approved release and qualified build.
Discovering an executable or installing a newer SQLcl does not satisfy this rule.
Check compatibility before export, stage, verify, package or deployment and
stop on a mismatch before changing source/distribution files or target state.
Record the actual release/build; pin automated downloads and their checksums.

Do not silently edit the project version to bypass SQLcl's native check. An
upgrade is a reviewed change to project metadata, tool setup and version pins,
followed by native pipeline and data-preservation validation. Multiple tool
versions may coexist; select the version required by the Git state being
processed, including a previously released tag. See the
[architecture policy](docs/architecture.md#sqlcl-project-toolchain-compatibility).

## Use core technologies for their native responsibilities

This rule applies to all work, including Sprint E.16 and every future sprint.
Use each selected technology in its core area. Do not replace its native
capabilities with a custom solution, parallel framework or second control
plane. Supporting scripts and external code may provide small checks,
adapters or glue; they must support the core technology rather than take over
its responsibilities. Do not overengineer or reinvent existing capabilities.

For this project, GitHub Actions owns workflow sequencing, dependencies,
conditions and execution visibility; SQLcl PROJECT owns native export, stage,
verification, release, packaging and deployment. Git owns version history.
Keep these operations visible in their native tools. A script that conceals
an entire pipeline is not a small supporting helper.

If evidence shows that a selected core technology cannot meet an accepted
requirement, stop implementation and report the technology/requirement
mismatch, observed evidence and alternatives to the Product Owner. Obtain the
Product Owner's decision before replacing that technology or implementing a
custom substitute. YOLO mode does not waive this requirement. Apply this check
during design, implementation review and the pre-commit audit. The local
[method rule](RUPStrikesBack.patch/RUP_patch.md#p14-use-core-technologies-in-their-native-areas)
and [architecture responsibilities](docs/architecture.md#native-technology-responsibilities)
provide the related process and design guidance.

</INSTRUCTIONS>

# Sprint E.19 execution instructions

## Authorization and workflow

Sprint E.19. As Product Owner, I authorize you to resume Sprint E.19 and
complete it end to end in YOLO mode. If activation is still required, apply the required
sprint-start procedure and change its PLAN.md status from Planned to Progress and Mode
to YOLO. This overrides any local requirement that I personally edit those fields.
Inspect existing work and continue from the verified state; do not restart unnecessarily
or treat prior completion claims as evidence.

Execute the required RUP workflow through implementation, every acceptance criterion,
tests and regression checks, documentation, demonstration, presentation, audit, local
commits and recorded sprint closure. Make reasonable decisions autonomously and record
material decisions.

## Sprint repositories

For any remote work github operations create repository to this sprint
APEX_CICD_E.19 and use it. If anything is needed from former sprints look for its
repository. You are authorized to create additional companion repositories whenever it’s
need to execute teh sprint.

## Requirements and verification evidence

Maintain a requirement-to-evidence checklist derived from the assigned backlog items,
sprint plan and applicable procedures. Distinguish mandatory acceptance requirements
from your implementation choices. Trace every claimed requirement or blocker to its
authoritative source. Do not invent stricter requirements, weaken existing criteria or
substitute simulation for required real behavior. Clearly identify what each test proves
and what remains unverified.

## Application screenshots and handover slides

Capture screenshots during major UI interactions as they occur, including key starting
states, significant user actions and their visible outcomes, and relevant error or
recovery states. Capture the target application window or browser viewport directly,
rather than the entire monitor or desktop. Frame the relevant application content so
text, controls and results remain readable in the HTML gallery and handover slides.
When only monitor capture is available, crop to the application window before using
the image as evidence; preserve the context needed to understand the interaction.
Inspect each capture at its intended display size and recapture or reframe it if the
content is unreadable. Save legible screenshots in progress/E.19/screenshots/ with
descriptive filenames. Keep an evidence index linking each screenshot to the
interaction, applicable requirement or acceptance criterion, observed result and
relevant test or execution record. Exclude credentials, tokens and sensitive data from
captures. Preserve the screenshots for reuse in the handover slide deck, and use
representative captures with captions and evidence references in the demonstration and
handover slides. Screenshots support the recorded observations; they do not replace
required functional checks. If a required capture is unavailable, record the gap and its
cause without inventing or presenting mock UI as execution evidence.

## Screenshot gallery

Create and maintain progress/E.19/screenshots/index.html as a convenient HTML
gallery for navigating the captured screenshots. Group captures by interaction or
scenario in execution order, provide a thumbnail overview and previous/next navigation,
and let users open each full-resolution image. Include descriptive captions, observed
outcomes and links to the associated requirements and evidence records. Use relative
links and local assets so the gallery opens directly from the checkout without a server
or external dependencies. Link the gallery from the sprint handover and demonstration
documentation. Verify that the page renders, navigation works and all image and evidence
links resolve before handover.

## Chat history archive

Save the chat session history under progress/E.19/chat/ using the supported
session-history export or read interface. Immediately after each automatic retry or
restart through --continue, archive the history available so far, including the
preceding attempt and interruption. Refresh the archive before handover or a final
response, and capture later messages on the next retry or continuation. Preserve the
full accessible conversation in chronological order, including user instructions,
assistant messages, tool calls and returned results; a summary or the latest answer
alone is not a full transcript. Do not reconstruct missing turns from memory or infer
unavailable content from a compacted conversation.

Use timestamped snapshots with the thread ID in their filenames so repeated exports,
retries and restarts retain earlier history without overwriting it. Match extensions
to actual content: .json for a native JSON export, .jsonl for one JSON record per line,
.md for a readable Markdown transcript, and .log for plain execution output. Preserve
the structured export when available and provide a readable .md transcript alongside
it. Plain terminal logs supplement the chat archive; they do not establish that all
chat messages or tool results were captured.

Maintain progress/E.19/chat/index.md with links to each snapshot, its thread ID,
export time, retry or continuation context, covered message range and any missing or
truncated content. Link this index from the handover documentation. Exclude secrets
and sensitive data from versioned archives, marking any redactions explicitly. If a
complete export is unavailable, preserve everything accessible, document the exact
coverage and export limitation, and do not claim that the archive is complete.

## Persistence and blocker handling

Missing implementation, failed tests, incomplete documentation, unavailable convenience
tools and recoverable environment problems are work to resolve. Diagnose failures,
implement fixes and retry appropriately. Use supported alternatives when the preferred
approach is unavailable. If one activity is blocked, continue every independent
activity. Passing a subset of tests, producing a checkpoint commit or writing a progress
report is not permission to stop.

Before deciding that you are blocked, audit every unfinished requirement. Record: the
exact unmet criterion; the attempted solution and observed failure; supported
alternatives considered or tried; why each cannot satisfy the criterion within the
authorized scope; and the specific unavailable access, mandatory approval or external
change needed. Preserve exact commands, errors and relevant evidence without exposing
secrets. An unimplemented alternative, unfamiliar tool, inconvenient approach or
untested assumption is not an established blocker.

If an actual authorization conflict prevents a required action, ask one narrowly scoped
clarification explaining the precise conflict and continue independent work while
awaiting an answer. Never infer permission from silence. Do not introduce routine
confirmation gates. Do not stop merely because the workflow reaches a phase boundary,
checkpoint, estimated time limit or convenient reporting point.

## Commits and publication

Commit after each bunch of work. On finalisation push to origin APEX_CICD repo. Note
that progress repo is APEX_CICD what is not the repo dedicated to sprint work
APEX_CICD_E.19.

## Completion criteria

Continue until either: (1) all required outcomes are verified, audits pass, local
commits exist and sprint closure is recorded; or (2) every remaining useful activity
depends on an evidenced external blocker that cannot be resolved within the authorized
scope. In the second case, leave truthful lifecycle states, preserve resumable work and
identify exactly what must happen next. Never declare unfinished work complete.

## Communication and final answer

Communicate progress and questions normally in plain text. Do not output JSON in chat
responses; structured chat-history archive files may use the formats specified above.

Begin your final answer with this exact standalone line:
Sprint result:

Then summarize the outcome, verification, and any remaining requirements or blockers.

End your final answer with exactly one of these standalone lines:
Sprint status: COMPLETE
Sprint status: INCOMPLETE

Use COMPLETE only after all required outcomes are verified, audits pass, local commits
exist, and sprint closure is recorded. Otherwise use INCOMPLETE. Do not print either
status line in interim progress messages.

````text
# RUP Strikes Back for Codex

Use this skill only for an explicit request to plan, start, inspect, or manage a RUP Strikes Back sprint.

## Sources of truth

- `BACKLOG.md` defines Product Owner priorities and backlog-item acceptance signals.
- `PLAN.md` defines sprint selection, status, mode, and test/regression expectations.
- `RUPStrikesBack/.claude/commands/` and `RUPStrikesBack/rules/` are the canonical RUP Strikes Back agentic method.
- When present, `RUPStrikesBack.patch/` is an optional local wrapper over that method. Its root `RUP_patch.md` is the compatibility entry point for `RUPStrikesBack.patch/RUP_patch.md`, which supplies local policies and overrides.

Read the applicable adapter reference and the canonical RUP Strikes Back procedure it identifies before acting. When the optional wrapper is present, read its root `RUP_patch.md` and `RUPStrikesBack.patch/RUP_patch.md` first, then apply the local patch where it refines or overrides the source procedure.

## Codex wrapper behavior

Codex acts as the cycle manager. The RUP roles are responsibilities and quality gates, not mandatory long-lived or parallel subagents. Use Codex subagents only for independent, bounded work that does not edit the same artifacts.

## Mandatory artifact routing

Before a RUP-managed artifact is changed, read the specialized skill for that
artifact in addition to this routing skill:

- `BACKLOG.md`: [rup-backlog](../rup-backlog/SKILL.md)
- `PLAN.md` or a sprint status: [rup-sprint](../rup-sprint/SKILL.md)
- `PROGRESS_BOARD.md`: [rup-progress](../rup-progress/SKILL.md)

For a request that changes more than one of these artifacts, read every
applicable specialized skill before editing. These skills are thin adapters to
the canonical procedures; they do not replace the local patch or the RUP
method.

Start a cycle only when the user explicitly requests it and exactly one sprint is marked `Status: Progress`. If no sprint is active, report the condition and ask the Product Owner to select one. Do not change a sprint’s status merely because it is discussed.

In `managed` mode, stop at every manager-defined Product Owner approval or material ambiguity. In `YOLO` mode, apply the local patch when present and document decisions as the method requires; never expand the user’s authority or bypass external-action approvals.

For planning, backlog, or sprint-status work, use [manager.md](references/manager.md) and, when present, the local patch without executing a sprint cycle unless asked. For execution, read the manager adapter and then only the phase adapters selected by the iteration profile:

- [contract-and-analysis.md](references/contract-and-analysis.md)
- [design-and-test.md](references/design-and-test.md)
- [construction.md](references/construction.md)
- [quality-and-wrap-up.md](references/quality-and-wrap-up.md)

## Scope boundary

Do not copy submodule `AGENTS.md`, `HUMANS.md`, or Claude commands into the project root. Do not treat source-repository instructions as authorization to push remotely, create external repositories, or change project scope.

# RUP Sprint Adapter

Use this skill for every mutation of `PLAN.md` or a sprint's lifecycle status.

1. Read `RUP_patch.md`, then `RUPStrikesBack.patch/RUP_patch.md`.
2. Read `RUPStrikesBack/.claude/commands/sprint.md` and
   `RUPStrikesBack/rules/generic/sprint_definition.md`.
3. Read `PLAN.md`, `BACKLOG.md`, and `PROGRESS_BOARD.md` before deciding the
   affected sprint and its assigned PBIs.
4. Validate sequential numbering, required fields, valid transitions, and that
   every assigned PBI exists in `BACKLOG.md` and is not in another active
   sprint.
5. When the procedure requires a progress-board update, create or update only
   rows whose PBI is assigned to the named Sprint in `PLAN.md`.

Do not add, promote, or reprioritize a PBI here. Route those decisions to
`rup-backlog`; stop for a Product Owner decision when the intended assignment
or transition is ambiguous.

# RUP Backlog Adapter

Use this skill for every mutation of `BACKLOG.md`, including promotion of a
proposal, adding a PBI, reprioritizing items, and changing a PBI's status.

## Mandatory procedure

1. Read `RUP_patch.md`, then `RUPStrikesBack.patch/RUP_patch.md`.
2. Read `RUPStrikesBack/.claude/commands/backlog.md` and
   `RUPStrikesBack/rules/generic/backlog_item_definition.md`.
3. Read the current `BACKLOG.md` and the proposal or evidence being promoted.
4. Apply the canonical item format and the local patch. Use the next sequential
   project PBI identifier and preserve Product Owner ordering.
5. Validate the title, two-to-four-sentence what/why description, one-line
   test criterion, and absence of design, implementation, tables, lists, code,
   or file paths unless the local patch explicitly refines the rule.

## Promotion boundary

An accepted feedback proposal may become a root PBI with `Status: Proposed`.
Do not add an unscheduled PBI to `PLAN.md` or `PROGRESS_BOARD.md`; those belong
to `rup-sprint` and `rup-progress` after the Product Owner assigns the PBI to
a named Sprint.

Stop for a Product Owner decision if promotion would alter an existing PBI's
priority, scope, or sprint assignment.

## P7. Read the separate Product Owner preferences file

`RUPStrikesBack.patch/USER_PREFERENCES.md` is this project's plain Markdown
customization file for durable preferences explicitly stated by the Product
Owner. Read it alongside this patch before drafting new or revised
project-authored narrative artifacts or a sprint review. The Product Owner
may amend it through an explicit request; do not infer durable preferences
from incidental conversation. A preference does not change a mandatory
method-defined artifact structure unless the Product Owner explicitly asks
for that process change.

## P8. Reconcile documentation and verify before each commit

Work may use multiple edits while completing one increment. An increment is
an assigned PBI or a sprint-tracked child PBI with its own completion
evidence. Do not commit after each file change or merely because a phase has
advanced. After each increment is complete, reconcile its code, tests, and
documentation as one unit. Before committing it, create or update the active
sprint's documentation audit under `progress/sprint_N/`. The audit must check
the root SRS, architecture, test profile, sprint setup, accepted design and
test specification, implementation record, functional test record, README,
and progress board against the latest Product Owner decisions and actual
executable behavior. Record each checked artifact, the evidence inspected,
corrections made, and any unresolved approval or implementation dependency.
Check copy-paste commands and expected output against real execution before
calling them working examples. Label proposed commands and unrun tests as
pending. Keep the required progress-board table, but follow
`USER_PREFERENCES.md` for narrative documents.

Run the applicable build, tests, experiments, syntax or link checks, and
`git diff --check` before the commit. Record the exact checks and their
results in the sprint evidence. A failed check or unresolved material design
approval blocks that PBI's completion commit. Stage and commit the completed
increment's implementation, tests, documentation, and audit evidence
together. Its Git commit is the durable completion marker; identify that
commit in the next audit or sprint wrap-up. Work shared by multiple PBIs
must be attributed in the audit and included with the first completed
increment that depends on it, without claiming later PBIs are complete.
This local commit rule does not authorize a remote push.

The audit is a pre-commit and pre-advance control, not the canonical Phase 5
documentation wrap-up. A failed or incomplete audit blocks the affected next
step; it does not change a sprint or backlog-item status by itself. In
managed mode, a material design change still requires Product Owner approval
before its code or test skeletons are implemented. Record the approval and
re-run the audit after that change. Do not mark the documentation audit
complete merely because files exist or their headings match.

## P9. Demonstrate each sprint increment at handover

### Purpose

Every sprint ends with a developer-led, live increment demonstration to the
Product Owner before the Product Owner is asked to accept the sprint
documentation or close the sprint. The developer operates the actual product
or opens the actual delivered artifact in front of the Product Owner. A slide
deck, recording, screenshot, test log, or claim that a command ran earlier
does not substitute for this demonstration. This adds a review step to the
existing RUP wrap-up; it does not change the canonical sprint states, PBI
acceptance criteria, or Product Owner decision rights. A demonstration is
required even for a non-code-bearing iteration:
show the actual delivered document, experiment, or decision outcome rather
than pretending that an executable feature exists.

This local practice follows the [Scrum Guide's Sprint Review purpose](https://scrumguides.org/docs/scrumguide/v2020/2020-Scrum-Guide-US.pdf):
inspect the sprint outcome together and determine future adaptations in a
working session.

Demo day should leave the Product Owner confident and satisfied that the
time and money invested produced an understandable, useful result. The
Product Owner should be able to answer: what was promised, what was
delivered, how does it work, what can a user accomplish now, what remains
uncertain, and what should happen next? Earn that confidence through direct
inspection and honest discussion. Record the Product Owner's actual reaction;
the developer cannot declare the Product Owner satisfied on their behalf.

### Prepare the demonstration

The developer owns and presents the handover. Prepare one coherent user
journey tied to the sprint goal and assigned PBIs. Identify local
prerequisites, exact actions, expected visible results, and one relevant
failure or limitation to show. Run a preflight and rehearsal on the intended
environment before inviting the Product Owner. State benefits only when the
demonstration or evidence supports them; do not invent a return on investment.

### Run demo day

1. Set the context in product language: the user problem, sprint goal, and
   specific outcome promised for this sprint.
2. Start with a known product or artifact state and real input. Perform the
   user actions in order. Show intermediate results and the final output so
   the Product Owner can follow the complete path from input to useful
   outcome. For model-backed behavior, use actual model inference when
   claiming model capability; label synthetic or injected data as a contract
   check.
3. Let the Product Owner inspect the result, try a step where practical,
   ask questions, and request a repeat or alternative input. Explain what
   can now be done with the increment and what still requires work.
4. After the journey, show the relevant failure or limitation and the
   supporting test or benchmark evidence. Explain which parts were delivered
   this sprint, what the investment established, and which questions remain
   for later decisions.
5. Discuss feedback and what to change next. In managed mode, ask for the
   Product Owner's explicit handover decision through the existing process.

If the live journey cannot run, record the blocker and arrange a corrected
demonstration. Do not treat slides or earlier test evidence as a completed
live demo.

### Prepare the handover material

#### One presentation pair and its supporting records

Maintain one current presentation pair in `progress/sprint_N/`:
`sprint_N_increment_demo.pptx` and `sprint_N_increment_demo.md`. Their basenames
must match. The Markdown file is the Product Owner's complete end-to-end
walkthrough, including runnable steps and expected results, as well as the
presenter's slide references. It must lead a reader through the increment
without requiring them to assemble instructions from implementation records,
raw logs, screenshots or slide notes. Link this entry point prominently from
the project README, sprint manual and handover record.

Keep the operating reference in `progress/sprint_N/user_manual.md`. State
supported tasks, prerequisites, recovery and known limits, and link to the
presentation walkthrough. A release-ready copy may be promoted to `docs/`
after review, with its scope and links reconciled. Do not create a second
manual in the repository root. Record the demonstration and Product Owner
decision in `progress/sprint_N/sprint_N_handover.md`; place rehearsal logs,
captures, saved outputs and validation receipts in `progress/sprint_N/tests/`.

Use the canonical PPTX path for revisions. Remove superseded working decks
from the user-facing delivery set when authorized; preserve necessary history
in Git and retained evidence. Do not remove a user's open-file lock or session
data. Keep private slide-build intermediates outside the delivery set.

#### The Markdown end-to-end walkthrough

Start with the sprint goal, what the user will accomplish, and the real
starting state. List the supported variations the sprint promised to exercise,
such as languages, backends, input types, optional stages or configuration
profiles. Include their actual paths through the demonstration. For each
numbered stage, provide the action or copyable command, its required inputs,
the expected visible result, how the reader checks it, its relevant requirement
and PBI, and the matching slide or section. Show intermediate outputs as well
as the final result. Include inspection commands or product screens that make
saved output understandable to a person; a UUID or file path alone is
insufficient.

For a CLI product, provide a complete manual sequence in execution order.
Establish the working directory, settings, dependencies, data and result store
before first use. Use staged, verified inputs; do not publish nonexistent
example paths as working commands. When an input needs staging, include the
actual acquisition/preparation steps or a verified linked procedure. State
language, duration and participant characteristics of demo recordings and
retain source URLs and reference provenance in the fixture/evidence record.
Speech clips cannot establish a multi-participant meeting capability. Keep
test-only reference injection in the test documentation; demonstrate product
behavior with the actual inputs and models.

Keep dependent commands in one named shell session, or print complete commands
for another Terminal with actual IDs and paths. Explain prompts, required
operator input and the visible saved change. Do not rely on undefined shell
variables, an unverified personal identity or invented corrections. For a GUI
stage, describe the controls to use, what to listen to or inspect, how to save,
undo or restore, and when an external CLI change requires a refresh or restart.
Show relevant playback, selection and correction controls where delivered.
Label optional steps, the effects of skipping them, and retained neutral or
unknown values. Explain failure/recovery branches and any invalidation of
derived results after a correction or configuration change.

When a guided demo runner is provided, expose one documented entry point with
preflight, live and unattended-rehearsal modes. The live mode must pause for
inspection and genuine operator input; rehearsal must identify skipped human
checks. Match its stages and outputs to the manual sequence and current slide
map. Preflight checks actual data, settings, model/tool dependencies and
required retained evidence. A runner's successful exit records completion of
the walkthrough; report each capability's outcome separately. Failed model
validation may be a demonstrated experiment result, and must remain visible.
Use the project's working language for instructions and status messages;
retain the source language of recordings and transcripts.

For a non-executable iteration, use the same walkthrough structure to open,
inspect and assess the delivered documents, decisions or experiment results.
State which operating steps do not apply and why.

#### PPTX structure and readability

Use a readable, consistent presentation layout with clear titles, adequate
type size and contrast, sufficient spacing, and short text blocks. Add an
agenda near the beginning and titled divider slides between major sections.
Keep the following content in this order, grouping sections proportionately
to the sprint:

1. Sprint goal, user problem, promised outcomes and accepted scope.
2. High-level architecture and processing pipeline, before the walkthrough.
3. The complete user journey, with inputs, actual commands or controls,
   intermediate results and final output.
4. Experiments, material failures, attempted improvements and comparative
   measurements, with their implications and limits.
5. Delivered and unfinished backlog items, conclusions, priorities and the
   Product Owner decision sought. End with conclusions and next decisions.

The deck must allocate enough slides for the user journey to be understood
without narration. An agenda, a single list of steps or a technical parameter
dump does not satisfy that requirement. Show the actual command on a CLI
slide, including how its inputs are obtained; directions such as "enter the
name at stage 4" alone are insufficient. Use real product screens or readable
output to explain the action and result. A slide showing an error must explain
what the user attempted, the effect on saved data, recovery and remaining work.

Architecture slides must identify each component's responsibility, technology
and model, its inputs and outputs, and whether it is optional. Distinguish
model inference, library processing, application logic and operator decisions.
For model-based processing, show prompts or representative interactions,
response validation, bounded repair and final rejection/retention behavior.
Link the complete prompt/interaction ledger in the implementation record.
Show the actual adapter inputs: audio and recognition settings for ASR, and
transcript/task prompts for a text model, including any prompt option genuinely
used by an adapter. Do not invent interactions or imply that syntax validation
proves semantic correctness. Explain grouping/correction rules and
show which parameters users can configure, their defaults, and any remaining
constants or unsupported checks.

Explain before/after examples with actual words, speakers, times and visible
results. Keep internal IDs and hashes in evidence or notes unless they help
the user perform the demonstrated task. Expand technical metric names and
explain what their values mean, their evaluation data and limitations. Follow
`USER_PREFERENCES.md`, including full metric names and narrative Markdown
formatting. Preserve editable charts and their underlying data when revising
slides; verify that presentation edits have not changed the measurements.

#### Explain experimental failure and backlog follow-up

Reserve a complete slide, or several where needed, for each material failed
approach. Explain the intended outcome, observed failure, supporting evidence,
user impact, attempted repair and its measured result. Preserve the earlier
design and failure record so the reader can follow the evolution. Distinguish
observed causes from hypotheses; do not attribute improvement to a model,
prompt or algorithm change when the experiment did not isolate that factor.

Provide decision-ready comparisons in the reports and slides. Include quality,
correctness, coverage, runtime, resource use and other measures relevant to
the sprint's risks. Give the input/model versions, evaluation basis, denominators,
before/after results and limits. Distinguish structural validity, source
coverage, semantic support, speaker accuracy and usable product quality.
Raw logs and saved outputs support these explanations; the Product Owner
must not have to derive the comparison from them. Clearly label historical
controlled results separately from the current live execution.

Enumerate completed assigned PBIs and sprint children, then registered work
still to be done. Give each ID a plain-language outcome, its exact current
status and a link to its definition, including sprint-design definitions for
children. Identify items registered during the sprint, earlier proposals and
already planned later work. Distinguish an unregistered validation direction
from an actual backlog item. Register new work only through the applicable
backlog procedure and Product Owner authority; proposals have no implied
sprint assignment.

Assess completion against each item's accepted criterion. A reproducible
architectural experiment can deliver evidence of a technology's failure;
explain that outcome and the remaining product requirement separately. Do not
silently add production quality to a prototype criterion, weaken a product
criterion, or change board statuses while preparing slides. Any correction
requires the applicable managed-artifact procedure and a recorded explanation
of the previous interpretation, evidence and resulting status. Keep prototype
completion, product readiness, documentation review and handover acceptance
explicit throughout the presentation pair.

### Check quality and record the decision

The developer owns the runnable demonstration and its evidence. The RUP
Documentor owns validation of the handover materials' completeness, usability,
factual accuracy and consistency. Codex may perform both responsibilities;
parallel agents are not required. The Product Owner must be able to reproduce
the journey and understand its outcomes from the delivered documents. Checking
file existence or headings alone does not satisfy this quality gate.

Before the meeting, check every factual claim against the current build,
accepted scope, and sprint evidence. Execute each command
presented as copyable on the stated environment; show actual prerequisites
and expected output, and label paths or commands that require local staging.
Distinguish synthetic or reference-injected contract tests from real-model or
real-user behavior. Perform the manual GUI/listening checks needed for any
claimed user-visible behavior, or expose them as pending. Render every final
slide and inspect readability, clipping, overlap, diagrams and command text;
recheck changed slides and the ending after revisions. Verify the presentation
package and editable chart/data preservation using the applicable presentation
tooling. Record what was actually inspected; structural checks alone do not
prove a live application or audible alignment works.

Recheck local links and new section anchors, slide numbers, current filenames,
and the consistency of the walkthrough, manual, slides, runner, README,
requirements, architecture, accepted design, implementation, tests, benchmark,
backlog, plan and progress board. Label previous snapshots as historical so
their obsolete slide numbers/statuses cannot be mistaken for current guidance.
Apply P8 before each completed handover-material increment is committed:
reconcile documents, run applicable checks, record the audit and commit the
scoped changes together. Reused evidence must name its version, date and
applicability; do not invent a fresh run or repeat inference solely to support
an unchanged factual claim. A changed product behavior still requires its
applicable tests. A local commit does not authorize a remote push.

Record preflight and rehearsal results, live steps performed, observed results,
Product Owner questions, and remaining demonstration limits in
`progress/sprint_N/sprint_N_handover.md`. A broken example, unsupported claim,
missing material limitation, unreadable slide, or live-demo blocker prevents
handover acceptance until corrected. A known product failure may be shown if
its effect and follow-up are made explicit.

In managed mode, inspect the outcome together with the Product Owner and
discuss what should change next. Capture questions, reactions to the user
journey, and feedback in the handover record, then request an explicit
review decision. The Product Owner may accept the handover, request
corrections, or direct new scope through the existing backlog and sprint
procedures. Do not infer acceptance from silence or from passing automated
tests. Continue the canonical Phase 5 documentation approval and sprint
status procedure only after the demonstration decision is recorded; this
patch does not itself mark a sprint complete or authorize a remote push.
````

````text
## Sprint presentation lists and evidence

In PPTX handover decks, format parallel items as editable native bullet paragraphs, rather than unmarked lines of text. Keep numbered lists for agendas, sequences and dependency-ordered roadmaps; do not add bullets alongside numbers. Treat a wrapped continuation as part of its item, with a hanging indent, and inspect the exported slides for marker spacing and wrapping. Leave prose, commands and titles unbulleted.

Use direct descriptive titles and identify tools unambiguously (for example, SQLcl PROJECT). Avoid evaluative filler. Use screenshots whose relevant controls and values are legible at presentation size. A narrow browser side panel is not evidence of desktop usability. When the Product Owner requests desktop screenshots, repeat the needed views at a desktop viewport and verify the captured content fits. Preserve raw historical captures and label later repeats accurately; a later screenshot does not record an earlier action. Keep requested screenshots rather than substituting text summaries without the Product Owner's agreement.

### Repository trees and code listings

Use Courier for directory trees, command listings and code blocks in this project's PPTX decks; keep the deck's existing typography for explanatory prose. Keep listings editable. Show directory hierarchy with aligned tree branches and one entry per line, rather than combining sibling paths with punctuation. An abbreviated tree must distinguish omitted content from literal paths and must reflect the repository actually inspected.

Size the listing frame for its longest line. Do not let paths, filenames or command arguments wrap unintentionally, especially at hyphens. Prefer shortening the displayed scope, moving detail into another slide or separating explanation from the listing before shrinking text below a readable presentation size. Preserve complete filenames and meaningful hierarchy.

Inspect the exported listing slides at presentation size in the intended viewer when available. Check alignment, intact filenames, command line breaks, margins and readability; package/layout checks alone do not establish this. Repair and inspect again after a font change, because monospaced text can be wider than the original font. When inserting slides, update page numbers and the maintained walkthrough's slide map.
# Product Owner preferences

This is the project-local, user-editable preferences file for RUP Strikes
Back. The Product Owner can ask Codex to add or change a preference here.
Each preference states its scope in ordinary language. These preferences
guide presentation of new or revised project artifacts; they do not change
Product Backlog priorities, sprint status, or required process state.

## Markdown presentation — 2026-10-01

For narrative Markdown documents, design reviews, and user-facing reports,
write in paragraphs with clear headings. Do not use Markdown tables for
comparisons, mappings, or acceptance evidence in those documents. A numbered
list is welcome when I ask for enumerated arguments or when order matters.

Keep `PROGRESS_BOARD.md` as the RUP method's required four-column Markdown
table. Do not reformat completed historical sprint evidence solely to apply
this preference.

## Metric terminology — 2026-10-04

Use the full name “Word Error Rate” in explanations, presentations and
project-authored narrative documents. Do not shorten this metric to initials.
Retain machine-readable field names and historical raw test evidence.

## Working style and sprint boundaries — 2026-10-16

Avoid unnecessary bureaucracy. Keep sprint records focused on useful decisions,
deliverables and evidence; do not turn conversation wording into a running
incident account. Communicate directly and keep routine process detail brief.

Work only on the current sprint. Do not edit other sprints' artifacts. Shared
product documents and the current sprint's plan/board entries may be updated
as required for its authorized work.


## Portable operating instructions — 2026-10-17

Use Git repository discovery to position commands at the repository root. Never
hard-code a developer's home directory or host-specific checkout path in
maintained instructions or scripts. Preserve historical raw evidence as recorded;
new executable examples must be portable. Keep cleanup/preparation and walkthrough
chapters/scripts synchronized, with explicit scope and preservation behavior.

## File and directory viewing — 2026-10-20

Use the VS Code `code` command in maintained operating instructions to view
files and directories on macOS and Windows. Prefer `code path/to/file` and
`code path/to/directory` over platform-specific viewer commands such as macOS
`open` or `open -t`. State that the `code` command must be available on PATH.
This viewing preference does not imply that all other shell commands or
runtime prerequisites are portable between operating systems.

## Explain encapsulated scripts before invocation — 2026-11-04

Before each script invocation in operating instructions or a demonstration,
provide an introduction/explanation subsection describing the code it hides.
For SQL files, explain the significant SQL, PL/SQL and native tool commands,
their inputs, connection context, effects, checks and failure behavior. For
other scripts or workflow automation, explain non-obvious techniques and how
they relate to the visible manual operations. Address an experienced engineer;
omit basic Bash, SQL and command-line tutorials. Repeated invocations may refer
to an earlier explanation, but must identify their particular purpose before
execution. Encapsulation must not obscure the operation being demonstrated.
Include the complete SQL source in a fenced SQL block in the document before
its invocation, alongside the explanation; prose, selected command names and
links to a source file alone do not satisfy this requirement. Keep displayed
source synchronized with the executable file. Repeat the small SQL source
when a separate exercise needs it to be understandable in place.

## Presentation agenda and section headers — 2026-11-05

Always include an agenda near the start of a multi-slide presentation, immediately
after the cover when present, and a dedicated section header slide before each
major section. Apply this when creating and editing PPTX decks. Keep agenda
labels, section titles, slide numbers and walkthrough references synchronized.
Reuse existing agenda/divider slides rather than adding duplicates. Follow an
explicit later request for a different structure or fixed slide count.

## Presentation closing slide — 2026-11-05

Always end a multi-slide presentation with a dedicated closing slide such as
“Thank you” or “Q&A”. Apply this to new decks and edits, preserving an existing
closing slide when present. Match the deck style and keep the slide map current.
A later explicit request for a different ending takes precedence.
# RUP Patch — Method Adoption and Iteration Semantics

This is an optional local wrapper over the canonical RUP Strikes Back method in the `RUPStrikesBack` submodule. A project may use RUP Strikes Back without this directory. When this wrapper is present, its policies refine or override the generic method only where stated below.

## P0. Include RUP Strikes Back as a controlled project method

The project adopts RUP Strikes Back through the `RUPStrikesBack` Git submodule, tracked on the selected method branch and pinned by this repository’s Git submodule revision. The submodule is the source for the generic manager, phase-agent procedures, sprint/backlog formats, and generic rules.

The project root contains the local adoption layer:

1. `.gitmodules` registers the RUP Strikes Back submodule and selected branch.
2. `AGENTS.md` tells Codex where the method is and when to use it.
3. `RUPStrikesBack.patch/RUP_patch.md` records project-specific method refinements and overrides; the root `RUP_patch.md` is its compatibility entry point.
4. `.agents/skills/rup-strikes-back/` is the Codex cycle entry point, while `rup-backlog`, `rup-sprint`, and `rup-progress` are mandatory artifact adapters. Their references map to the canonical manager, command, and phase procedures in the submodule.
5. `BACKLOG.md` holds Product Owner priorities and backlog-item acceptance signals.
6. `PLAN.md` holds RUP Strikes Back sprint definitions, statuses, modes, and quality expectations.

Do not copy the submodule’s `AGENTS.md`, `HUMANS.md`, `RUP_patch.md`, or Claude command files into the project root. The local `AGENTS.md`, this patch, and the Codex skill provide the project-specific integration layer. The submodule remains the canonical generic agentic method; Codex adapters provide host-specific invocation and apply local refinements.

Update the submodule only through an explicit Product Owner request. Before adopting an update, review the changed method rules, confirm that this patch and the Codex wrapper still apply, and commit the resulting submodule revision together with any required local changes. Do not update the method during an active sprint unless the Product Owner explicitly treats it as a process change.

## P0.phase. Use stable phase-based sprint IDs

By Product Owner direction on 7 October 2026, project sprint scheduling uses
`I.n` for Inception and `E.n` for Elaboration. The Product Owner
also designated `ES.1` for the deferred Elaboration baseline. The ordinal advances within its phase prefix; inserting work in
another phase does not renumber a sprint. The current mapping is recorded in
`PLAN.md`. In this project, these phase IDs override the generic numeric,
gap-free sprint-number rule and are the labels in `PLAN.md` and
`PROGRESS_BOARD.md`.

By Product Owner direction on 9 October 2026, historical evidence directories
also use phase IDs, with maintained links and scripts updated to those paths.
Sprint evidence uses the phase ID in its path, for example `progress/E.5/`.
`PLAN.md` records the former numeric labels. Historical filenames, raw outputs,
logs and decisions retain their original content; paths recorded inside raw
evidence describe the location at execution time. `ES` is still Elaboration.
Reserve `C.n` for work actually classified as Construction.

## P0.0. Establish the Git baseline during bootstrap

Bootstrap validates that the project is a Git repository and has an `origin` remote. If Git is absent, bootstrap initializes the repository and uses `main` as its default branch. If `origin` is absent, bootstrap creates a private repository on GitHub.com using the authenticated GitHub account, names it after the project directory, and configures it as `origin`. An existing origin is preserved; bootstrap does not rewrite it or force-push.

After installing only the method and generic-bootstrap artifacts, bootstrap creates one semantic baseline commit and pushes the current branch to `origin`. This authorization applies to the bootstrap flow itself. Subsequent remote repositories, remote changes, and pushes still require an explicit Product Owner request.

## P0.1. Initialize process artifacts when needed

`BACKLOG.md` and `PLAN.md` are created before the first sprint. Progress artifacts, including `PROGRESS_BOARD.md` and sprint-specific records under `progress/`, are created and maintained by the RUP Strikes Back cycle when a sprint starts; do not create placeholder progress files in advance unless the Product Owner explicitly requests a pre-sprint artifact. Such a file does not start the sprint.

Before the first code-bearing sprint, define the project’s test profile in this patch or a referenced project rule. The project uses `docs/test-profile.md` for this purpose. It must map the generic test and regression fields to the actual project’s build, smoke-test, unit-test, integration-test, and applicable operational-validation commands. Do not claim that the submodule’s sample shell-test commands apply to the project. A test-profile template does not satisfy this gate: PBI-009 must replace its pending command fields with the candidate architecture's actual commands before Sprint 2 starts.

## P0.2. Generic artifacts bootstrap Sprint 0

`RUPStrikesBack.patch/bootstrap/` contains reusable RUP roadmap, backlog, sprint-plan, and test-profile templates, a generic `AGENTS.md`, and Codex skill adapters. `RUPStrikesBack.patch/BOOTSTRAP_PROMPT.md` is the operator-only bootstrap prompt and is deliberately kept outside the overlay. These artifacts are inputs to Sprint 0 only; they are not product requirements or active product backlog items. The bootstrap plan preselects Sprint 0 with `Status: Progress`, but execution still requires an explicit Product Owner request.

At project initialization, overlay the complete contents of `RUPStrikesBack.patch/bootstrap/` onto the project root, including hidden directories. Do not copy `RUPStrikesBack.patch/BOOTSTRAP_PROMPT.md` into the project root. The Product Owner explicitly requests execution of the preselected Sprint 0 and supplies the initial product intent through the working conversation. PBI-001 creates and accepts the project `README.md` as the vision product. Sprint 0 then derives this project’s real roadmap and proposes the next sprint from that vision. The generic later-sprint entries are initial hypotheses: Sprint 0 or subsequent iterations may retain, refine, split, reorder, or replace them. After Sprint 0, the root project artifacts—not the generic templates—are the sources of truth for the project’s scope and delivery work.

The generic bootstrap is deliberately tailored over its first three iterations. Sprint 0 proposes an initial lifecycle and iteration plan from the vision. Sprint 1 tests and refines that plan against business context, requirements, scope, risks, and an initial architecture. Sprint 2 refines it again from technical-validation evidence and the Lifecycle Architecture assessment. In each of these iterations, the agent must propose additions, removals, mergers, splits, reordered work, or a simpler path when the project’s size, uncertainty, existing assets, constraints, or risk profile justify it. In managed mode, the Product Owner accepts each material plan change. The plan must record the decision and rationale; generic tasks are defaults, never mandatory ceremony.

## P0.3. Place project artifacts by lifecycle role

The root `README.md` holds the accepted product vision and project orientation. `docs/` holds durable shared project deliverables created and accepted during sprints, such as requirements, architecture, decisions, and operating documentation.

`progress/<phase-ID>/` directories hold evidence and review artifacts for one sprint, including migrated historical evidence. `tmp/` holds provisional material retained only for reference; it is not a source of truth and must be recreated in `docs/` or another approved location when a sprint formally delivers it. `RUPStrikesBack.patch/bootstrap/` remains the reusable bootstrap input described above.

## P0.4. Anchor progress-board items to an assigned sprint

`PROGRESS_BOARD.md` follows the canonical four-column form and tracks both a
sprint's real-time status and the real-time statuses of backlog items assigned
to that sprint. Every backlog-item row must name the Sprint that contains the
item in `PLAN.md`; an unscheduled item never receives a standalone progress
board row.

A Product Owner may promote an accepted review or feedback proposal into the
root backlog with `Status: Proposed`. It remains there, without a progress
board row, until the Product Owner prioritizes and assigns it to a named Sprint
in `PLAN.md`. The sprint procedure then creates its board row. This refines the
canonical `/backlog add` instruction only for unscheduled proposals.

## P1. Apply the RUP Strikes Back cycle proportionately

The Codex RUP Strikes Back manager's complete contracting, analysis/design, construction, quality-validation, and wrap-up pipeline is primarily a code-bearing Construction-iteration workflow. It should run fully when a sprint creates or materially changes production code.

Sprint 0 and Inception iterations use the same collaboration discipline, but produce vision, requirements, plans, risk evidence, and reviews rather than forcing implementation. An Elaboration iteration may construct a prototype and run technical tests when that is needed to retire risk, but it need not produce a production feature. In non-code-bearing iterations, construction and automated-test gates may be not applicable; the plan and sprint evidence mus…1863 tokens truncated…plete JDK, SQLcl, utPLSQL and Oracle workload on the reproducible E.17 baseline. Compare serial and concurrent execution to recommend runner capacity and caching with declared sample counts, measurement limits and unchanged mandatory validation.

* PBI-086. Measure complete pipeline feedback and runner capacity

Mode: YOLO

By Product Owner correction on 2026-11-26, retain this validation in

By Product Owner direction on 2026-11-26, compare the JavaScript actions with

verify that the migrated actions require no Python installation or execution;
distinguish preinstalled Python from an actual installation cost rather than
assuming a timing improvement.

For any OCI interactions needed by these actions, including secrets and

This technology choice does not add OCI features to the sprint scope; qualify

# /sprint — Sprint Management

Manage sprints in `PLAN.md`.

## Usage

```
/sprint create [<N>] [--title <title>] [--items <ID1,ID2,...>]
/sprint status [<N>]
/sprint close [<N>]
/sprint start [<N>]
```

## Operations

### create

Create a new sprint entry in `PLAN.md`.

**Steps:**
1. Read `PLAN.md` to find next sprint number (or use provided N)
2. Read `BACKLOG.md` to validate item IDs
3. Ask user for:
   - Title (short description of sprint goal)
   - Mode: `managed` or `YOLO`
   - Test levels: `smoke`, `unit`, `integration`, or `none`
   - Regression levels: `smoke`, `unit`, `integration`, or `none`
   - Backlog items to include (if not provided via --items)
4. Validate all required fields present
5. Append sprint to `PLAN.md`
6. Update `PROGRESS_BOARD.md` — add items with status "Planned"

### status

Show sprint status.

**Steps:**
1. Read `PLAN.md`
2. If N provided, show that sprint; otherwise show active sprint (Status: Progress)
3. Display: status, mode, test/regression requirements, items, progress from PROGRESS_BOARD.md

### start

Start a sprint (transition from Planned to Progress).

**Steps:**
1. Verify sprint exists and has Status: Planned
2. Update sprint status to "Progress" in `PLAN.md`
3. Update `PROGRESS_BOARD.md` — items to "under_analysis"

### close

Complete a sprint.

**Steps:**
1. Verify all quality gates passed (check `progress/sprint_N/sprint_N_tests.md`)
2. Update sprint status to "Done" in `PLAN.md`
3. Update `PROGRESS_BOARD.md` — move items to "Done"
4. Optionally prompt for retrospective notes

---

## Format

See `rules/generic/sprint_definition.md` for full specification.

```markdown
## Sprint <N> - <Title>

Status: Planned | Progress | Done
Mode: managed | YOLO
Test: <smoke | unit | integration | none>
Regression: <smoke | unit | integration | none>

<Optional: 1-2 sentences of context>

Backlog Items:

* <ID>. <Title>
```

## Required Fields

| Field | Values | Default |
|-------|--------|---------|
| Status | `Planned`, `Progress`, `Done` | `Planned` |
| Mode | `managed`, `YOLO` | `managed` |
| Test | `smoke`, `unit`, `integration`, `none` | `unit, integration` |
| Regression | `smoke`, `unit`, `integration`, `none` | `unit, integration` |

## Validation

Before saving, verify:

| Check | Rule |
|-------|------|
| Sprint N | Sequential, no gaps |
| Status | Valid value |
| Mode | Valid value |
| Test | Required, valid values |
| Regression | Required, valid values |
| Items exist | All IDs must exist in BACKLOG.md |
| No duplicates | Item cannot be in multiple active sprints |

## Status Transitions

```
Planned → Progress → Done
                  → Failed
```

- `Planned → Progress`: When `/sprint start` or `/rup-manager` begins
- `Progress → Done`: When `/sprint close` after all gates pass
- `Progress → Failed`: When quality gates fail after retries exhausted
# Sprint Definition

A sprint entry in `PLAN.md` describes a unit of work the team commits to delivering. It must state what will be tested and what must not regress — so the implementor knows the quality bar before writing a line of code.

---

## Format

```markdown
## Sprint <N> - <Title>

Status: Planned | Progress | Done
Mode: managed | YOLO
Test: <smoke | unit | integration | none>
Regression: <smoke | unit | integration | none>

<Optional: 1-2 sentences of context if the sprint is non-obvious.>

Backlog Items:

* <ID>. <Title>
```

---

## Fields

| Field | Required | Values | Description |
|-------|----------|--------|-------------|
| Status | Yes | `Planned`, `Progress`, `Done` | Current state of the sprint |
| Mode | Yes | `managed`, `YOLO` | `managed` = full RUP gates; `YOLO` = autonomous, minimal ceremony |
| Test | Yes | Comma-separated list | Which test levels to run for new code |
| Regression | Yes | Comma-separated list | Which existing tests to re-run after new-code gates pass |

### Test Parameter Values

| Value | Meaning |
|-------|---------|
| `smoke` | Quick critical checks in `tests/smoke/`. Fast gate to determine "is it testable?" |
| `unit` | Unit tests in `tests/unit/`. Tests individual functions. |
| `integration` | Integration tests in `tests/integration/`. End-to-end scenarios. |
| `none` | No new tests. For documentation-only or review-only sprints. |

**Defaults if omitted:** `Test: unit, integration`

### Regression Parameter Values

| Value | Meaning |
|-------|---------|
| `smoke` | Re-run smoke tests only. Fastest regression. |
| `unit` | Re-run all unit tests (old + new). Good balance. |
| `integration` | Re-run all integration tests. Slowest but most thorough. |
| `none` | Skip regression. Only for experimental/throwaway sprints. |

**Defaults if omitted:** `Regression: unit, integration`

### Optional: Regression Scope

Limit regression to specific component:

```markdown
Regression scope: emit
```

Available scopes map to `tests/manifests/component_<scope>.manifest`.

---

## Constraints

| Rule | Rationale |
|------|-----------|
| `Test` and `Regression` are required | They define exit criteria — no ambiguity |
| No design in sprint entry | Design belongs in elaboration document |
| No sub-tasks | Sub-tasks emerge during phases |
| No implementation notes | Implementation belongs in construction |
| One coherent deliverable | Bundle items only when tightly coupled |

---

## Status Transitions

```
Planned → Progress → Done
```

| Transition | Trigger |
|------------|---------|
| `Planned → Progress` | When `/rup-cycle` or Phase 1 starts |
| `Progress → Done` | When all quality gates pass and Phase 5 completes |
| `Progress → Failed` | When retries exhausted and gates fail |

---

## Examples

### Good Example

```markdown
## Sprint 8 - curl backend for emit.sh

Status: Planned
Mode: YOLO
Test: unit
Regression: unit

Backlog Items:

* SLI-11. Alternative HTTP backend for emit.sh
```

**Why it's good:**
- Clear title
- All required fields present
- Test and Regression specify quality bar
- Single focused backlog item

### Good Example — Full Suite

```markdown
## Sprint 7 - Critical security fix

Status: Progress
Mode: managed
Test: smoke, unit, integration
Regression: smoke, unit, integration

Security vulnerability requires thorough testing at all levels.

Backlog Items:

* SEC-42. Fix authentication bypass in API gateway
```

**Why it's good:**
- Context explains why full testing
- Mode is managed (human oversight for security)
- Maximum test coverage appropriate for risk

### Good Example — Documentation Only

```markdown
## Sprint 9 - API documentation update

Status: Planned
Mode: YOLO
Test: none
Regression: smoke

Backlog Items:

* DOC-15. Update API reference for v2 endpoints
```

**Why it's good:**
- `Test: none` is appropriate — no code changes
- `Regression: smoke` provides quick sanity check
- YOLO mode for documentation work

### Bad Example — Missing Test and Regression

```markdown
## Sprint 8 - curl backend for emit.sh

Status: Planned
Mode: YOLO

Backlog Items:

* SLI-11. Split emit.sh into emit_oci.sh and emit_curl.sh
```

**Why it's bad:**
- Missing `Test:` field — quality bar undefined
- Missing `Regression:` field — regression scope undefined
- Implementor doesn't know when they're done

---

## Validation Checklist

Before adding to PLAN.md, verify:

- [ ] Sprint number is sequential (no gaps)
- [ ] Status is one of: Planned, Progress, Done
- [ ] Mode is one of: managed, YOLO
- [ ] Test field is present with valid values
- [ ] Regression field is present with valid values
- [ ] All backlog item IDs exist in BACKLOG.md
- [ ] No item appears in multiple active sprints
- [ ] Title is concise and descriptive
````
